Biotechnology English

A high-level biotechnology English curriculum for platform science, assay design, translational evidence, biomarkers, IP, partnerships, investor updates, regulatory-adjacent planning, and scientific-business dialogue.

For biotech scientists, translational researchers, assay-development teams, platform teams, program managers, alliance managers, business-development staff, and biotech executives.

What you will practice · Choose your pace

What you will practice

  • Distinguish a measured platform signal from a proposed mechanism and a broader development hypothesis.
  • Explain within-run precision separately from between-day variation and request a traceable investigation.
  • Specify the scope of biomarker validation and distinguish measurement performance from response prediction.
  • Summarize a nonclinical finding with its design and limitations, then identify missing information for the next review.
  • Distinguish titer, recovered quantity, product quality, and scale-up evidence in a process-transfer discussion.

Choose your pace

Quick practice · 15 minutes: complete one short exchange, check the explanations, and read both roles aloud.

Extended practice · two or three sittings: check the case facts, study the vocabulary and phrases, complete the longer conversation, then rehearse the follow-up exchange.

Level guide: designed for intermediate to advanced learners. B1 learners can start with the short exchanges and supplied phrases; B2 learners can complete the cloze conversations; C1 learners can focus on the distinctions in the language checks. These are teaching suggestions, not a certified level assessment.

Prompts

Original fictional language practice, not experimental instructions or medical, regulatory, legal, or investment advice. All organizations, candidates, numerical examples, and negotiations are invented. Apply current scientific standards, local procedures, appropriate professional review, and the actual governing agreements.

01Platform Technology and Scientific ThesisOne cell line, three proposed diseases+

Your goal

Distinguish a measured platform signal from a proposed mechanism and a broader development hypothesis.

This lesson in the book, page 4 ↗

A Read the situation

One cell line, three proposed diseases

Research lead Nia reviews a fictional platform presentation with venture lead Leo. Three independent preparations of one cell line show an average reporter-signal reduction of 40% against the stated control. No orthogonal functional result or data from other disease systems are available. The slide claims proven activity across three diseases. The immediate request is funding for a bounded follow-up study, not approval for clinical use. Nia and Leo must retain the promising observation while correcting the unsupported breadth.

Nia: Research lead / Leo: Venture lead

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. Which evidence is supplied?

Answer and explanation

D. A 40% average reporter-signal reduction in three independent preparations of one cell line

The brief supplies a reporter result in one cell line, not cross-disease or clinical evidence.

2. Which claim exceeds that evidence?

Answer and explanation

D. Proven activity across three diseases

The additional disease systems have not been tested in the supplied case.

3. What is the immediate decision request?

Answer and explanation

D. Funding a bounded follow-up study

The stated request concerns a limited next research study, not a clinical or rights decision.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Three independent preparations provide information about variation within the tested biological system. They are not three diseases or three clinical trials. A reporter signal can support a research direction without establishing the full mechanism or proving human benefit.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
platform technology
A reusable technological approach intended to support multiple applications.develop a platform technology
scientific thesis
The evidence-based argument and hypotheses underlying a research program.articulate the scientific thesis
modality
A type of therapeutic or technological approach.compare therapeutic modalities
target
The biological entity a program aims to affect.define the target
target engagement
Evidence that an intervention interacts with its intended biological target.assess target engagement
mechanism of action
The biological process through which an intervention produces an effect.investigate the mechanism of action
reporter signal
A measurable output used as an indicator in an experimental system.quantify a reporter signal
cell line
A maintained population of cells used for research.characterize the cell line
biological replicate
An independently prepared biological sample or experimental unit.include biological replicates
technical replicate
A repeated measurement of the same biological material or preparation.distinguish technical replicates
control condition
A comparison used to interpret an experimental result.specify the control condition
orthogonal assay
A different measurement approach used to examine the same underlying question.run an orthogonal assay
functional readout
A measurement of a relevant biological activity or outcome.confirm a functional readout
assay interference
An effect on the measurement system that can distort the apparent result.investigate assay interference
counter-screen
A separate screen used to identify unwanted or misleading effects.include a counter-screen
selectivity
Preference for the intended target or effect relative to alternatives.assess selectivity
off-target effect
An effect involving an unintended biological target or pathway.evaluate off-target effects
proof of concept
Evidence supporting feasibility within a specified scope.define the proof-of-concept scope
proof of mechanism
Evidence supporting the proposed causal biological mechanism.seek proof of mechanism
generalizability
The extent to which a finding applies beyond the tested setting.assess generalizability
disease model
An experimental system representing selected aspects of a disease.qualify the disease model
translational hypothesis
A proposed link between experimental findings and a later development setting.test a translational hypothesis
evidence gap
A missing piece of information relevant to a claim or decision.identify an evidence gap
decision gate
A defined point at which evidence is reviewed against criteria.set a decision gate

An orthogonal assay uses a different measurement approach to examine the underlying question; a technical repeat of the same readout is not automatically orthogonal. Define the relevant follow-up and decision criteria without assuming its result in advance.

C Notice the language

Precision can strengthen a pitch

A confident scientific presentation distinguishes observation, interpretation, and the next test. Qualifying a claim does not require burying the result. Put the system and limitation beside the headline so a listener can understand both the promise and the reason for the next study.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Lead with the observation
We observed a 40% average reduction in the reporter signal.
Name the system
The data come from three independent preparations of one cell line.
Limit the breadth
Other disease systems have not yet been tested.
Separate signal from function
The reporter result does not yet establish the functional effect.
Keep the hypothesis
The broader platform application remains a testable hypothesis.
Request another method
We need a different readout to examine the underlying question.
Check interference
Could the signal reflect an effect on the assay rather than the intended biology?
Describe the control
The percentage is relative to the control specified in this experiment.
Avoid a mechanism claim
We have not established the full mechanism from this result alone.
State the opportunity
The signal is promising enough to justify a focused next study.
Identify the gap
The missing evidence is functional confirmation in a relevant additional system.
Define the decision
The next study should answer whether this application merits further development.
Keep replication precise
These are independent preparations, not repeated reads presented as independent samples.
Correct the slide
Replace proven across three diseases with the result actually measured.
Bound the ask
We are requesting the next research step, not clinical authorization.
Close with criteria
Agree what evidence would support or stop the next stage.
6 language and precision notes
Promising
Signals potential without claiming an established development outcome.
Proven
Requires the claim's scope to match the supporting evidence.
Platform
A reusable ambition does not itself demonstrate every application.
Replicate
Specify whether the repeat is technical, biological, or across runs.
Reduction
Name the readout and comparison; do not let a percentage imply patient benefit.
Orthogonal
Different measurement logic matters more than a new label on the same assay.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which slide headline is best supported?

Answer and explanation

B. Reporter signal reduced by an average 40% in the tested cell line; broader applications remain untested.

The supported headline retains the observed result and its actual experimental boundary.

2. One preparation is read three times with the same method. How should those reads be described?

Answer and explanation

D. Three technical measurements of one biological preparation

Repeated reads of the same material are technical replication. They do not create independently prepared biological units, change the measurement approach, or establish new disease applications.

3. Why request an orthogonal functional readout?

Answer and explanation

C. To examine the biological question through a different measurement approach

A different approach can test whether the original signal reflects the intended biology.

4. Which funding request matches the brief?

Answer and explanation

D. Support a bounded follow-up addressing functional confirmation and broader relevance.

The brief asks for a next research study that addresses the stated evidence gaps.

Check explanations in the book, page 85 ↗

E Conversations

One cell line, three proposed diseases

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Nia: Research lead / Leo: Venture lead

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • decision gate
  • generalizability
  • translational hypothesis
  • evidence gap
  • proof of mechanism
  • assay interference
  • orthogonal assay
  • reporter signal
  • biological replicate
  • scientific thesis
  1. Leo
    The headline says our platform has proven activity across three diseases. It sounds strong, but I want to know whether each part of that claim is supported.
  2. Nia
    We have a 1 reduction averaging forty percent against the specified control. The work used three independent preparations of one cell line, not three disease systems.
  3. Leo
    Can we still call it a platform result? The investors need to see why this is more than a single isolated observation with no development direction.
  4. Nia
    Yes, as evidence relevant to the 2. We should explain the proposed applications while separating what we observed from what we expect to test next.
  5. Leo
    The slide currently turns the three preparations into a visual with three disease labels. I can see how that could imply that each disease has been tested.
  6. Nia
    Keep the 3 description explicit. Three independent preparations tell us something about that system; they do not create evidence for three separate indications.
  7. Leo
    What would you say about the mechanism? The lower signal looks consistent with the proposed pathway, but the headline makes the connection sound settled.
  8. Nia
    We do not yet have 4. The reporter finding supports investigation, but an effect on the measurement system could also influence the apparent result.
  9. Leo
    What would the next study tell us that another reporter run would not? The funding request needs a scientific question, not just a larger sample count.
  10. Nia
    It should include an 5 addressing the underlying biology. A different measurement approach would help distinguish a relevant biological effect from a feature of this reporter system.
  11. Leo
    We should be clear that you are asking to test that distinction, not promising that the second method will confirm the first. Otherwise the milestone is misleading.
  12. Nia
    Correct. We also need to assess 6 and the appropriate controls. A promising decrease is not sufficient to dismiss competing explanations for the measured change.
  13. Leo
    How do we describe the proposed disease expansion without making it sound like a result? I want an ambitious but defensible sentence in the investor discussion.
  14. Nia
    Call it a 7 and name the additional systems we propose to evaluate. The audience can understand the opportunity without being told that unperformed work has succeeded.
  15. Leo
    I will keep the forty-percent observation in the main line and put the tested system beside it. The expansion will be a proposed application, not a proven one.
  16. Nia
    That makes the 8 visible. We still lack functional confirmation and data in other disease systems, so those limits should travel with the headline.
  17. Leo
    For the decision itself, we want a bounded research budget, not a commitment to take every suggested indication into development. The proposal needs a stopping point.
  18. Nia
    Set a 9 with agreed evidence criteria. We should know what question the work will answer and how that answer affects the next development choice.
  19. Leo
    I will remove the three-disease claim and request the bounded follow-up. Keep the forty-percent result beside the actual cell system so the claim cannot drift again.
  20. Nia
    Good. That preserves the platform ambition while keeping 10 open to investigation. We can be confident about the observation without claiming that every future application already works.

Answers and explanations
  1. reporter signal Turn 2. The reporter signal is the measured experimental output, not a direct demonstration of clinical benefit.
  2. scientific thesis Turn 4. The scientific thesis connects the observation to a research argument without treating every hypothesis as established.
  3. biological replicate Turn 6. A biological replicate is an independent preparation within the tested system, not a different disease application.
  4. proof of mechanism Turn 8. Proof of mechanism requires evidence for the proposed biological explanation beyond the supplied reporter observation.
  5. orthogonal assay Turn 10. An orthogonal assay uses a different measurement approach to examine the underlying biological question.
  6. assay interference Turn 12. Assay interference can alter the measured signal without establishing the intended biological effect.
  7. translational hypothesis Turn 14. A translational hypothesis proposes a link to another development setting that remains to be tested.
  8. evidence gap Turn 16. The evidence gap is the missing functional and broader-system support needed for the larger claim.
  9. decision gate Turn 18. A decision gate defines when the follow-up evidence will be reviewed against agreed continuation criteria.
  10. generalizability Turn 20. Generalizability concerns application beyond the tested setting, which the supplied evidence has not yet established.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Leo: The headline says our platform has proven activity across three diseases. It sounds strong, but I want to know whether each part of that claim is supported.
  2. Nia: We have a reporter signal reduction averaging forty percent against the specified control. The work used three independent preparations of one cell line, not three disease systems.
  3. Leo: Can we still call it a platform result? The investors need to see why this is more than a single isolated observation with no development direction.
  4. Nia: Yes, as evidence relevant to the scientific thesis. We should explain the proposed applications while separating what we observed from what we expect to test next.
  5. Leo: The slide currently turns the three preparations into a visual with three disease labels. I can see how that could imply that each disease has been tested.
  6. Nia: Keep the biological replicate description explicit. Three independent preparations tell us something about that system; they do not create evidence for three separate indications.
  7. Leo: What would you say about the mechanism? The lower signal looks consistent with the proposed pathway, but the headline makes the connection sound settled.
  8. Nia: We do not yet have proof of mechanism. The reporter finding supports investigation, but an effect on the measurement system could also influence the apparent result.
  9. Leo: What would the next study tell us that another reporter run would not? The funding request needs a scientific question, not just a larger sample count.
  10. Nia: It should include an orthogonal assay addressing the underlying biology. A different measurement approach would help distinguish a relevant biological effect from a feature of this reporter system.
  11. Leo: We should be clear that you are asking to test that distinction, not promising that the second method will confirm the first. Otherwise the milestone is misleading.
  12. Nia: Correct. We also need to assess assay interference and the appropriate controls. A promising decrease is not sufficient to dismiss competing explanations for the measured change.
  13. Leo: How do we describe the proposed disease expansion without making it sound like a result? I want an ambitious but defensible sentence in the investor discussion.
  14. Nia: Call it a translational hypothesis and name the additional systems we propose to evaluate. The audience can understand the opportunity without being told that unperformed work has succeeded.
  15. Leo: I will keep the forty-percent observation in the main line and put the tested system beside it. The expansion will be a proposed application, not a proven one.
  16. Nia: That makes the evidence gap visible. We still lack functional confirmation and data in other disease systems, so those limits should travel with the headline.
  17. Leo: For the decision itself, we want a bounded research budget, not a commitment to take every suggested indication into development. The proposal needs a stopping point.
  18. Nia: Set a decision gate with agreed evidence criteria. We should know what question the work will answer and how that answer affects the next development choice.
  19. Leo: I will remove the three-disease claim and request the bounded follow-up. Keep the forty-percent result beside the actual cell system so the claim cannot drift again.
  20. Nia: Good. That preserves the platform ambition while keeping generalizability open to investigation. We can be confident about the observation without claiming that every future application already works.
Dialogue in the book, page 10 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

One experimental system, not several diseases

Fictional platform review: a signal was observed in one experimental system, while an investor slide claims broad disease applicability.

  1. Scientific founder: The slide says our platform will work across several diseases. Does the experiment establish that?
  2. Research lead: No. We observed a promising signal in one system, not broad disease translatability.
  3. Scientific founder: Could we call it proof of concept while keeping the broader opportunity visible?
  4. Research lead: Yes, if we define exactly what concept and experimental setting the evidence supports.
  5. Scientific founder: The modality is the same across the proposed programs. Doesn't that reduce the uncertainty?
  6. Research lead: It may inform our hypothesis, but a shared modality doesn't demonstrate the same outcome everywhere.
  7. Scientific founder: How should I describe the broader thesis without presenting the future work as completed?
  8. Research lead: Call it a development hypothesis and identify the additional systems we still need to study.
  9. Scientific founder: I'll narrow the claim and keep the observed result separate from the proposed applications.
  10. Research lead: I'll review the experimental description so the platform story remains ambitious without overstating the evidence.
A delivery result is not a therapeutic outcome

Separate fictional research discussion: a platform delivered a reporter into a laboratory cell model; no therapeutic payload or patient outcome was tested.

  1. Business lead: The reporter entered the cells. Can we say the therapeutic modality has demonstrated benefit?
  2. Platform scientist: No. This experiment evaluated delivery of a reporter, not a therapeutic payload or patient outcome.
  3. Business lead: Then what part of the platform thesis does the result actually support?
  4. Platform scientist: A specific delivery capability in this cell model under the tested conditions.
  5. Business lead: Could we describe that as a limited proof of concept rather than a failed therapeutic study?
  6. Platform scientist: Exactly. The study didn't ask the therapeutic question, so neither success nor failure on that question is established.
  7. Business lead: What should we say about translatability when partners ask whether it will work clinically?
  8. Platform scientist: That remains to be investigated. Explain the evidence boundary and the proposed next questions.
  9. Business lead: I'll use a delivery-specific headline and remove the implied patient-benefit claim.
  10. Platform scientist: Good. I'll keep the reporter, model, and experimental scope explicit in the supporting explanation.
An observed effect with an uncertain mechanism

Separate fictional platform meeting: an experimental effect is repeatable within the supplied study, but the proposed mechanism has not been isolated.

  1. Program director: The effect repeated. Can we now tell the board that our proposed mechanism is confirmed?
  2. Discovery scientist: The repeated observation supports the effect in this study, but the mechanism hasn't been isolated.
  3. Program director: Isn't reproducing an effect the same as explaining why it occurs in the system?
  4. Discovery scientist: No. An observation and a causal explanation require different evidence, even when the observation is consistent.
  5. Program director: How do we avoid making the platform sound directionless while preserving that distinction?
  6. Discovery scientist: Present the mechanism as our working thesis and explain the tests intended to distinguish alternative explanations.
  7. Program director: Should we change the modality description because the mechanism remains uncertain?
  8. Discovery scientist: Only if the description itself is inaccurate. Don't revise established terminology simply to mask an evidence gap.
  9. Program director: I'll separate the repeatable effect from the unconfirmed explanation on the slide.
  10. Discovery scientist: I'll review those statements and keep any claim about broader translatability explicitly provisional.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

One cell line, three proposed diseases
  1. Read the slide-review exchange. State 40% average reporter reduction, three independent preparations, and one cell line together.
  2. Swap roles. Contrast a technical repeat with an orthogonal method; retain the untested broader applications.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

One model is not every model

A screening result is observed in one cell model. Two other proposed models have not been tested. The team requests a study of those additional systems.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Scientist: "The signal was observed in one ___."

Answer and explanation

B. model

The supplied result belongs to one tested cell model only.

2. Partner: "The other two are still ___."

Answer and explanation

A. untested

The briefing states that no testing has occurred in those additional systems.

3. Scientist: "Broader application remains a ___."

Answer and explanation

B. hypothesis

The proposed extension is a testable idea rather than an established result.

4. Partner: "The next study should address that evidence ___."

Answer and explanation

B. gap

The missing data concern whether the finding extends beyond the tested setting.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

Lesson 01: Platform Technology and Scientific Thesis
Original fictional case: Research lead Nia reviews a fictional platform presentation with venture lead Leo. Three independent preparations of one cell line show an average reporter-signal reduction of 40% against the stated control. No orthogonal functional result or data from other disease systems are available. The slide claims proven activity across three diseases. The immediate request is funding for a bounded follow-up study, not approval for clinical use. Nia and Leo must retain the promising observation while correcting the unsupported breadth.
Language workshop: One cell line, three proposed diseases
Communication goal: Distinguish a measured platform signal from a proposed mechanism and a broader development hypothesis.
Language guidance: Three independent preparations provide information about variation within the tested biological system. They are not three diseases or three clinical trials. A reporter signal can support a research direction without establishing the full mechanism or proving human benefit.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): We observed a 40% average reduction in the reporter signal. / The data come from three independent preparations of one cell line. / Other disease systems have not yet been tested. / The reporter result does not yet establish the functional effect. / The broader platform application remains a testable hypothesis. / We need a different readout to examine the underlying question. / Could the signal reflect an effect on the assay rather than the intended biology? / The percentage is relative to the control specified in this experiment. / We have not established the full mechanism from this result alone. / The signal is promising enough to justify a focused next study. / The missing evidence is functional confirmation in a relevant additional system. / The next study should answer whether this application merits further development. / These are independent preparations, not repeated reads presented as independent samples. / Replace proven across three diseases with the result actually measured. / We are requesting the next research step, not clinical authorization. / Agree what evidence would support or stop the next stage.
Vocabulary: platform technology: A reusable technological approach intended to support multiple applications. / scientific thesis: The evidence-based argument and hypotheses underlying a research program. / modality: A type of therapeutic or technological approach. / target: The biological entity a program aims to affect. / target engagement: Evidence that an intervention interacts with its intended biological target. / mechanism of action: The biological process through which an intervention produces an effect. / reporter signal: A measurable output used as an indicator in an experimental system. / cell line: A maintained population of cells used for research. / biological replicate: An independently prepared biological sample or experimental unit. / technical replicate: A repeated measurement of the same biological material or preparation. / control condition: A comparison used to interpret an experimental result. / orthogonal assay: A different measurement approach used to examine the same underlying question. / functional readout: A measurement of a relevant biological activity or outcome. / assay interference: An effect on the measurement system that can distort the apparent result. / counter-screen: A separate screen used to identify unwanted or misleading effects. / selectivity: Preference for the intended target or effect relative to alternatives. / off-target effect: An effect involving an unintended biological target or pathway. / proof of concept: Evidence supporting feasibility within a specified scope. / proof of mechanism: Evidence supporting the proposed causal biological mechanism. / generalizability: The extent to which a finding applies beyond the tested setting. / disease model: An experimental system representing selected aspects of a disease. / translational hypothesis: A proposed link between experimental findings and a later development setting. / evidence gap: A missing piece of information relevant to a claim or decision. / decision gate: A defined point at which evidence is reviewed against criteria.
Speaking task: Read the slide-review exchange. State 40% average reporter reduction, three independent preparations, and one cell line together. Swap roles. Contrast a technical repeat with an orthogonal method; retain the untested broader applications. Read the corrected one-model transfer. Keep two proposed models untested and the wider claim a hypothesis.
Partner: Nia: Research lead / Leo: Venture lead
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A screening result is observed in one cell model. Two other proposed models have not been tested. The team requests a study of those additional systems.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Leo: For the decision itself, we want a bounded research budget, not a commitment to take every suggested indication into development. The proposal needs a stopping point.
Nia: Set a decision gate with agreed evidence criteria. We should know what question the work will answer and how that answer affects the next development choice.
Leo: I will remove the three-disease claim and request the bounded follow-up. Keep the forty-percent result beside the actual cell system so the claim cannot drift again.
Nia: Good. That preserves the platform ambition while keeping generalizability open to investigation. We can be confident about the observation without claiming that every future application already works.
END REFERENCE
Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Lesson 01: Platform Technology and Scientific Thesis
Original fictional case: Research lead Nia reviews a fictional platform presentation with venture lead Leo. Three independent preparations of one cell line show an average reporter-signal reduction of 40% against the stated control. No orthogonal functional result or data from other disease systems are available. The slide claims proven activity across three diseases. The immediate request is funding for a bounded follow-up study, not approval for clinical use. Nia and Leo must retain the promising observation while correcting the unsupported breadth.
Language workshop: One cell line, three proposed diseases
Communication goal: Distinguish a measured platform signal from a proposed mechanism and a broader development hypothesis.
Language guidance: Three independent preparations provide information about variation within the tested biological system. They are not three diseases or three clinical trials. A reporter signal can support a research direction without establishing the full mechanism or proving human benefit.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): We observed a 40% average reduction in the reporter signal. / The data come from three independent preparations of one cell line. / Other disease systems have not yet been tested. / The reporter result does not yet establish the functional effect. / The broader platform application remains a testable hypothesis. / We need a different readout to examine the underlying question. / Could the signal reflect an effect on the assay rather than the intended biology? / The percentage is relative to the control specified in this experiment. / We have not established the full mechanism from this result alone. / The signal is promising enough to justify a focused next study. / The missing evidence is functional confirmation in a relevant additional system. / The next study should answer whether this application merits further development. / These are independent preparations, not repeated reads presented as independent samples. / Replace proven across three diseases with the result actually measured. / We are requesting the next research step, not clinical authorization. / Agree what evidence would support or stop the next stage.
Vocabulary: platform technology: A reusable technological approach intended to support multiple applications. / scientific thesis: The evidence-based argument and hypotheses underlying a research program. / modality: A type of therapeutic or technological approach. / target: The biological entity a program aims to affect. / target engagement: Evidence that an intervention interacts with its intended biological target. / mechanism of action: The biological process through which an intervention produces an effect. / reporter signal: A measurable output used as an indicator in an experimental system. / cell line: A maintained population of cells used for research. / biological replicate: An independently prepared biological sample or experimental unit. / technical replicate: A repeated measurement of the same biological material or preparation. / control condition: A comparison used to interpret an experimental result. / orthogonal assay: A different measurement approach used to examine the same underlying question. / functional readout: A measurement of a relevant biological activity or outcome. / assay interference: An effect on the measurement system that can distort the apparent result. / counter-screen: A separate screen used to identify unwanted or misleading effects. / selectivity: Preference for the intended target or effect relative to alternatives. / off-target effect: An effect involving an unintended biological target or pathway. / proof of concept: Evidence supporting feasibility within a specified scope. / proof of mechanism: Evidence supporting the proposed causal biological mechanism. / generalizability: The extent to which a finding applies beyond the tested setting. / disease model: An experimental system representing selected aspects of a disease. / translational hypothesis: A proposed link between experimental findings and a later development setting. / evidence gap: A missing piece of information relevant to a claim or decision. / decision gate: A defined point at which evidence is reviewed against criteria.
Speaking task: Read the slide-review exchange. State 40% average reporter reduction, three independent preparations, and one cell line together. Swap roles. Contrast a technical repeat with an orthogonal method; retain the untested broader applications. Read the corrected one-model transfer. Keep two proposed models untested and the wider claim a hypothesis.
Partner: Nia: Research lead / Leo: Venture lead
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A screening result is observed in one cell model. Two other proposed models have not been tested. The team requests a study of those additional systems.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Leo: For the decision itself, we want a bounded research budget, not a commitment to take every suggested indication into development. The proposal needs a stopping point.
Nia: Set a decision gate with agreed evidence criteria. We should know what question the work will answer and how that answer affects the next development choice.
Leo: I will remove the three-disease claim and request the bounded follow-up. Keep the forty-percent result beside the actual cell system so the claim cannot drift again.
Nia: Good. That preserves the platform ambition while keeping generalizability open to investigation. We can be confident about the observation without claiming that every future application already works.
END REFERENCE
Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

02Assay Development and ReproducibilityTight wells, shifting days+

Your goal

Explain within-run precision separately from between-day variation and request a traceable investigation.

This lesson in the book, page 13 ↗

A Read the situation

Tight wells, shifting days

Assay scientist Imani and project lead Chen review a fictional control sample measured over three days. Each day has a within-run coefficient of variation of 5%, but the day means are 80, 100, and 125 signal units. The same nominal control level was intended each day. The summary says fully reproducible. Reagent-lot, calibration, and operator records still need comparison. No acceptance limits or confirmed cause are supplied; the team must describe the variation without inventing a validation decision.

Imani: Assay scientist / Chen: Project lead

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What is consistent within each run?

Answer and explanation

B. The stated coefficient of variation is 5%.

The briefing gives a five-percent within-run coefficient of variation on each day.

2. What changes across days?

Answer and explanation

A. The mean signal: 80, 100, and 125 units

The day means differ even though the within-run variation is stated as the same.

3. What cannot yet be concluded?

Answer and explanation

A. A confirmed cause or full validation status

Neither a cause nor acceptance criteria sufficient for a validation decision are provided.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

A 5% within-run coefficient of variation describes relative spread inside that run. It does not establish matching means across days or accuracy against a reference. Without supplied acceptance criteria, the exercise cannot declare the assay validated or failed.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
assay development
Designing and refining a measurement method for a specified purpose.plan assay development
repeatability
Precision under closely matched conditions over a short interval.assess repeatability
intermediate precision
Precision with relevant changes within one laboratory, such as day or operator.evaluate intermediate precision
reproducibility
Precision under specified changed conditions, commonly across laboratories.define reproducibility conditions
within-run variation
Differences among measurements within a single analytical run.quantify within-run variation
between-day variation
Differences associated with measurements on different days.investigate between-day variation
coefficient of variation
Standard deviation divided by the mean, often expressed as a percentage.report the coefficient of variation
standard deviation
A measure of how observations are spread around their mean.calculate the standard deviation
mean signal
The average measured output for a defined group.compare mean signals
precision
Closeness among repeated measurements under stated conditions.assess analytical precision
accuracy
Closeness of a measurement to the relevant reference or accepted value.evaluate analytical accuracy
bias
A systematic difference from the relevant reference value.investigate measurement bias
calibration
Establishing the relationship between measurement response and reference values.verify calibration records
reference standard
A characterized material used to support measurement comparisons.qualify a reference standard
quality-control sample
A sample used to monitor analytical performance.review quality-control samples
reagent lot
An identified production batch of a reagent.compare reagent lots
lot-to-lot variation
Differences associated with separate batches of a material.evaluate lot-to-lot variation
plate effect
Variation associated with a measurement plate or its positions.assess a plate effect
edge effect
A position-related difference at the outer wells of a plate.check for an edge effect
assay drift
A change in measurement performance over time.monitor assay drift
normalization
Adjusting results to a defined comparison or reference basis.document normalization
acceptance criterion
A predefined requirement for judging a result or process.specify acceptance criteria
root-cause investigation
A structured effort to identify supported underlying explanations.conduct a root-cause investigation
run metadata
Information describing the conditions and history of an analytical run.preserve run metadata

Intermediate precision examines relevant changes within a laboratory; reproducibility often refers to broader changed conditions. Specify the intended meaning. Normalization must have a justified, documented basis, not be invented after the fact solely to make disagreeing runs appear aligned.

C Notice the language

Ask which kind of consistency

A colleague may use reproducible as a broad compliment while an assay specialist means a defined set of conditions. Clarify the tested source of variation rather than argue about the word alone. Numbers become useful when the comparison, units, and experimental structure are visible.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Separate the levels
The within-run precision is consistent, but the day means differ.
Give the values
The means are eighty, one hundred, and one hundred twenty-five signal units.
Define the statistic
The CV describes relative spread within each run.
Limit the conclusion
That does not establish agreement across days.
Ask for the criteria
Which predefined acceptance criteria apply to this intended use?
Check the comparison
Was the same nominal control level used in each run?
Preserve the raw result
Keep the original measurements and their run records.
Investigate conditions
Compare reagent lots, calibration, operator, and relevant run conditions.
Avoid premature blame
We have not established which factor explains the shift.
Explain precision
Tight measurements can still differ systematically from a reference.
Name the percentage base
Twenty-five above one hundred is a 25% increase relative to one hundred.
Challenge the label
Fully reproducible is broader than these data support.
Keep normalization justified
State the reference and method used for any adjustment.
Request traceability
Link the plotted mean to its raw data and metadata.
Report the status
Between-day variation is under investigation.
Close with the question
The next review must explain the shift and evaluate it against the agreed criteria.
6 language and precision notes
Precise versus accurate
Close repeated values may still be systematically offset from a reference.
CV
Expand coefficient of variation before using the abbreviation in a mixed-audience meeting.
Same mean
Equal spread does not require equal averages.
Drift
A descriptive possibility, not a confirmed mechanism for these three values.
Reproducible
Specify what was repeated and which conditions changed.
Failed
A formal judgment requires the applicable criteria, not only an unfavorable-looking chart.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which statement fits the data?

Answer and explanation

B. Each run has 5% CV, while day means differ from 80 to 125 units.

The correct statement keeps within-run spread separate from the observed between-day differences.

2. Relative to day two's mean of 100, how much higher is day three's mean of 125?

Answer and explanation

A. 25%

The increase is twenty-five divided by the reference value one hundred, giving twenty-five percent.

3. Which follow-up best supports investigation?

Answer and explanation

A. Compare traceable run conditions and raw data before assigning a cause.

Comparing records and raw data can test explanations without prematurely choosing one.

4. A colleague rescales each day to a mean of 100 and calls the assay reproducible. What remains unresolved?

Answer and explanation

D. Whether the adjustment has a justified basis and the original between-day variation is understood

Equalizing the plotted means is the adjustment's result, not independent evidence of performance. The original shift and the scientific basis for normalization still require examination.

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

  1. Chen
    Every run has a five-percent CV, so I kept fully reproducible in the summary. What am I missing before we show this to the project team?
  2. Imani
    The 1 is similar, but the means are eighty, one hundred, and one hundred twenty-five. The runs do not sit at the same level.
  3. Chen
    So the points can be tightly grouped each day while the groups move? I treated the repeated percentage as evidence of an unchanged result.
  4. Imani
    The 2 is standard deviation divided by mean. Five percent describes relative spread; it does not tell us that the means match.
  5. Chen
    We intended the same nominal control level. Could the newest operator explain the shift? That was the first suggestion in yesterday's meeting.
  6. Imani
    Let us compare the 3 first: operator, reagent lot, calibration and relevant conditions. We do not yet know which changes coincide.
  7. Chen
    Right. If the operator and reagent lot changed together, the pattern would not isolate either one. I should not report an attribution we cannot defend.
  8. Imani
    Keep both as questions in the 4. The point is to test explanations against the records, not settle on the most convenient person to blame.
  9. Chen
    All three runs were in this laboratory. Is between-day reproducibility the clearest heading, or would another term make the conditions more precise?
  10. Imani
    We are examining 5 within this laboratory. State the days and conditions examined so the reader does not infer an interlaboratory study.
  11. Chen
    I also wrote that day three rose twenty percent from day two. I divided twenty-five by the final value of one hundred twenty-five.
  12. Imani
    Use day two as the base. The 6 increased twenty-five percent: twenty-five divided by one hundred. Twenty percent describes the reverse decrease.
  13. Chen
    Thanks. Is the shift enough to call the assay failed? I cannot find the intended-use limits in this review pack.
  14. Imani
    We need the applicable 7. Do not invent a pass or failure threshold from this chart; report the between-day difference as under investigation.
  15. Chen
    Someone offered to rescale every day's mean to one hundred. It would remove the visible shift, but would it resolve the performance question?
  16. Imani
    Not by itself. Any 8 needs a justified reference and documented method. An adjustment chosen only to align these means can hide what needs investigation.
  17. Chen
    Then retain the original readings and show how any adjusted result was derived. The unadjusted plot should not disappear from the package.
  18. Imani
    Agreed. Include the 9 records too, so we can trace the measurement basis rather than infer it from the graph alone.
  19. Chen
    I will show the three means beside their CVs, correct the percentage, and list the missing criteria and records. No validation conclusion yet.
  20. Imani
    That gives 10 its proper scope. We retain the useful within-run result while making the unresolved between-day performance visible.

Answers and explanations
  1. within-run variation Turn 2. Within-run variation describes spread inside each run, not whether the averages match across different days.
  2. coefficient of variation Turn 4. The coefficient of variation expresses relative spread as standard deviation divided by the mean.
  3. run metadata Turn 6. Run metadata identifies conditions such as operator, reagent lot, and calibration that can support investigation.
  4. root-cause investigation Turn 8. A root-cause investigation tests supported explanations rather than assigning responsibility from an incomplete pattern.
  5. intermediate precision Turn 10. Intermediate precision covers relevant variation within one laboratory, including between-day conditions.
  6. mean signal Turn 12. Mean signal is the average measured output being compared between day two and day three.
  7. acceptance criterion Turn 14. An acceptance criterion supplies the predefined requirement needed for a formal performance judgment.
  8. normalization Turn 16. Normalization requires a defined and justified reference basis rather than an adjustment chosen only to conceal disagreement.
  9. calibration Turn 18. Calibration records describe the relationship to reference values and may help explain measurement differences.
  10. precision Turn 20. Precision concerns agreement among repeated measurements under specified conditions and must be qualified by those conditions.

Dialogue in the book, page 19 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Within-run consistency versus between-day reproducibility

Fictional assay review: results are consistent within one run but differ across three days; no cause has been established.

  1. Assay scientist: The repeats agree within one run. Can we describe the assay as fully reproducible?
  2. Quality scientist: Not yet. Results differ across three days, so the within-run evidence doesn't cover that variability.
  3. Assay scientist: Should we compare the daily averages alone to choose the best-performing day?
  4. Quality scientist: We should compare the runs under the same criteria and inspect controls and conditions, not select a favorable day.
  5. Assay scientist: What tradeoff should we explain if a faster method has only within-run testing?
  6. Quality scientist: It may save time per run, but between-day reproducibility remains an unresolved part of the assessment.
  7. Assay scientist: Would stronger sensitivity automatically outweigh that uncertainty for every intended assay use?
  8. Quality scientist: No. The intended use and agreed performance criteria determine what evidence is relevant.
  9. Assay scientist: I'll present within-run consistency separately and compare the three days transparently.
  10. Quality scientist: I'll review the comparison plan without assigning a cause or declaring the assay fully reproducible in advance.
A sensitivity comparison at different thresholds

Separate fictional assay discussion: method A and method B were evaluated using different positivity thresholds and different sample panels.

  1. Laboratory manager: Method A has the higher reported sensitivity. Should we choose it over method B?
  2. Assay developer: The thresholds and sample panels differ, so those headline results aren't a fair comparison yet.
  3. Laboratory manager: Could we keep each method's preferred threshold and simply rank the reported percentages?
  4. Assay developer: We need a justified comparison under the intended-use criteria, with threshold choices and sample differences visible.
  5. Laboratory manager: What else belongs beside sensitivity when we explain the options to the team?
  6. Assay developer: Specificity and reproducibility, plus practical requirements such as sample needs and turnaround time.
  7. Laboratory manager: We don't have comparable values for all those measures in this exercise.
  8. Assay developer: Then mark them unknown. Don't assume an unreported characteristic favors either method.
  9. Laboratory manager: I'll request a common evaluation plan before describing A as the superior assay.
  10. Assay developer: I'll outline the comparison and explain the tradeoffs without treating one attractive metric as the whole decision.
Faster preparation with a sample-volume tradeoff

Separate fictional assay selection: method C uses half the preparation time but twice the sample volume of method D; comparable analytical performance is not yet established.

  1. Research operations lead: Method C is faster. Can we replace method D for all samples next week?
  2. Assay scientist: C halves preparation time but needs twice the sample volume; analytical comparability is still unestablished.
  3. Research operations lead: So the operational advantage may matter differently when the available sample is limited?
  4. Assay scientist: Exactly. We should compare both methods for the same intended samples and performance criteria.
  5. Research operations lead: Could we assume equal specificity because both methods measure the same target?
  6. Assay scientist: No. A shared target doesn't establish equal analytical performance or reproducibility.
  7. Research operations lead: I'll show preparation time and sample volume as known differences, with performance marked pending.
  8. Assay scientist: Please also avoid presenting the proposed replacement date as an approved method change.
  9. Research operations lead: Agreed. I'll ask the review team which comparison evidence is needed before a decision.
  10. Assay scientist: I'll prepare that assessment so the eventual choice reflects the actual tradeoff rather than speed alone.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Tight wells, shifting days
  1. Read the assay review. State the 5% within-run CV alongside daily means of 80, 100, and 125.
  2. Swap roles. Correct the denominator: 100 to 125 is a 25% increase; 125 to 100 is a 20% decrease.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

A percentage needs its reference

Two comparable fictional runs have means of 120 and 150 units. Their within-run CVs are both 4%. No acceptance criteria are supplied.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Analyst: "The mean rose by ___ units."

Answer and explanation

D. thirty

Subtracting one hundred twenty from one hundred fifty gives thirty units.

2. Reviewer: "Relative to 120, that is a ___ increase."

Answer and explanation

A. 25%

Thirty divided by the reference value one hundred twenty equals twenty-five percent.

3. Analyst: "The four-percent CV describes within-run ___."

Answer and explanation

B. spread

The coefficient of variation concerns relative spread, not the change between means.

4. Reviewer: "A formal acceptance decision still needs the applicable ___."

Answer and explanation

D. criteria

The briefing supplies no acceptance criteria against which to make that decision.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Lesson 02: Assay Development and Reproducibility
Original fictional case: Assay scientist Imani and project lead Chen review a fictional control sample measured over three days. Each day has a within-run coefficient of variation of 5%, but the day means are 80, 100, and 125 signal units. The same nominal control level was intended each day. The summary says fully reproducible. Reagent-lot, calibration, and operator records still need comparison. No acceptance limits or confirmed cause are supplied; the team must describe the variation without inventing a validation decision.
Language workshop: Tight wells, shifting days
Communication goal: Explain within-run precision separately from between-day variation and request a traceable investigation.
Language guidance: A 5% within-run coefficient of variation describes relative spread inside that run. It does not establish matching means across days or accuracy against a reference. Without supplied acceptance criteria, the exercise cannot declare the assay validated or failed.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): The within-run precision is consistent, but the day means differ. / The means are eighty, one hundred, and one hundred twenty-five signal units. / The CV describes relative spread within each run. / That does not establish agreement across days. / Which predefined acceptance criteria apply to this intended use? / Was the same nominal control level used in each run? / Keep the original measurements and their run records. / Compare reagent lots, calibration, operator, and relevant run conditions. / We have not established which factor explains the shift. / Tight measurements can still differ systematically from a reference. / Twenty-five above one hundred is a 25% increase relative to one hundred. / Fully reproducible is broader than these data support. / State the reference and method used for any adjustment. / Link the plotted mean to its raw data and metadata. / Between-day variation is under investigation. / The next review must explain the shift and evaluate it against the agreed criteria.
Vocabulary: assay development: Designing and refining a measurement method for a specified purpose. / repeatability: Precision under closely matched conditions over a short interval. / intermediate precision: Precision with relevant changes within one laboratory, such as day or operator. / reproducibility: Precision under specified changed conditions, commonly across laboratories. / within-run variation: Differences among measurements within a single analytical run. / between-day variation: Differences associated with measurements on different days. / coefficient of variation: Standard deviation divided by the mean, often expressed as a percentage. / standard deviation: A measure of how observations are spread around their mean. / mean signal: The average measured output for a defined group. / precision: Closeness among repeated measurements under stated conditions. / accuracy: Closeness of a measurement to the relevant reference or accepted value. / bias: A systematic difference from the relevant reference value. / calibration: Establishing the relationship between measurement response and reference values. / reference standard: A characterized material used to support measurement comparisons. / quality-control sample: A sample used to monitor analytical performance. / reagent lot: An identified production batch of a reagent. / lot-to-lot variation: Differences associated with separate batches of a material. / plate effect: Variation associated with a measurement plate or its positions. / edge effect: A position-related difference at the outer wells of a plate. / assay drift: A change in measurement performance over time. / normalization: Adjusting results to a defined comparison or reference basis. / acceptance criterion: A predefined requirement for judging a result or process. / root-cause investigation: A structured effort to identify supported underlying explanations. / run metadata: Information describing the conditions and history of an analytical run.
Speaking task: Read the assay review. State the 5% within-run CV alongside daily means of 80, 100, and 125. Swap roles. Correct the denominator: 100 to 125 is a 25% increase; 125 to 100 is a 20% decrease. Read the corrected transfer. Preserve the 120 reference, 30-unit change, 25% increase, and missing acceptance criteria.
Partner: Imani: Assay scientist / Chen: Project lead
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Two comparable fictional runs have means of 120 and 150 units. Their within-run CVs are both 4%. No acceptance criteria are supplied.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Chen: Then retain the original readings and show how any adjusted result was derived. The unadjusted plot should not disappear from the package.
Imani: Agreed. Include the calibration records too, so we can trace the measurement basis rather than infer it from the graph alone.
Chen: I will show the three means beside their CVs, correct the percentage, and list the missing criteria and records. No validation conclusion yet.
Imani: That gives precision its proper scope. We retain the useful within-run result while making the unresolved between-day performance visible.
END REFERENCE

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.
