Original fictional case: Process scientist Hana and manufacturing lead Ben review two fictional 1-liter runs after a process transfer. Both achieved a harvest titer of 0.8 grams per liter. A 200-liter run has not been performed. The slide promises 160 grams of finished acceptable product by multiplying titer by volume. Downstream recovery and relevant quality results at the larger scale are unestablished. The team must retain the small-run result while correcting the forecast and defining the missing scale-up evidence.
Language workshop: Two small runs do not prove the large process
Communication goal: Distinguish titer, recovered quantity, product quality, and scale-up evidence in a process-transfer discussion.
Language guidance: At the same assumed titer, 0.8 grams per liter times 200 liters gives 160 grams at harvest. It does not establish downstream recovery, final purity, potency, or release. Titer is a concentration; always define the basis when using yield.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Both results came from one-liter runs. / The measured value is harvest titer, not released final quantity. / At the same titer, two hundred liters would contain one hundred sixty grams at harvest. / The larger process has not yet demonstrated that titer. / Downstream losses are not included in that calculation. / Quantity alone does not establish purity or potency. / CMC covers chemistry, manufacturing, and controls. / Which quality attributes need comparison after this change? / Two small runs do not establish the complete larger-scale process. / Mixing and mass transfer may differ with scale. / We need relevant larger-scale performance and quality data. / Route the process change through the appropriate quality review. / Which aspects of the large process does the small model represent? / Present this as a conditional harvest estimate. / A projected quantity is not a release decision. / Document the observed performance, open risks, and next agreed evaluation.
Vocabulary: CMC: Chemistry, manufacturing, and controls: the product and process quality package. / technology transfer: Movement of process knowledge and capability between teams or sites. / upstream processing: The production stages that generate the biological material. / downstream processing: Stages that recover and purify material after production. / titer: Product concentration at a specified process stage. / yield: Output relative to a stated input or theoretical basis, with context required. / recovery: The proportion of material retained through a specified process step. / purity: The proportion or profile of intended material relative to impurities. / potency: A measure of biological activity relevant to the product's function. / aggregation: Association of molecules into larger assemblies. / critical quality attribute: A property needing control within appropriate limits to ensure desired quality. / critical process parameter: A process variable whose variability can affect a critical quality attribute. / process parameter: A measurable or controlled operating variable in a process. / process characterization: Study of how process conditions affect performance and quality. / scale-up: Increasing the size or production capacity of a process. / scale-down model: A smaller model intended to represent relevant larger-process behavior. / mass transfer: Movement of a substance between regions or phases of a system. / mixing: Distribution of material within a process vessel or system. / hold time: The time material remains at a specified stage or condition. / bioburden: The level of viable microorganisms associated with a material or process. / comparability: Assessment that a change does not adversely affect relevant product quality, safety, or efficacy. / change control: Formal review and management of a proposed change. / process validation: Documented evidence that a process can consistently deliver the intended quality. / batch record: The record of materials, steps, observations, and events for a batch.
Speaking task: Read the scale-up exchange. Distinguish observed one-liter titer from assumed 200-liter performance and harvest from finished output. Swap roles. State 0.8 times 200 equals 160 grams before downstream recovery; do not claim release. Read the corrected transfer. Say fifty grams at harvest and forty after the stated step, with quality assessment separate.
Partner: Hana: Process scientist / Ben: Manufacturing lead

Optional second-round challenge: A hypothetical harvest contains 0.5 grams per liter in 100 liters. A stated downstream step retains 80% of the harvest material. No release decision is supplied.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Ben: One last wording point: two matching titers do not support saying comparability demonstrated. We still lack the relevant larger-scale quality results.
Hana: Exactly. Establishing comparability requires examining the consequences for the product. A single matching output is not the whole assessment.
Ben: The revised slide will say two one-liter runs at 0.8 grams per liter; 160 grams is hypothetical harvest output before recovery and quality evaluation.
Hana: That is a usable CMC update. Keep finished acceptable output unconfirmed and name the remaining evidence needed before making that commitment.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.

06IP, Freedom to Operate, and CollaborationA confidentiality agreement is not a material license+

Your goal

Identify unresolved material-use and publication terms while keeping ownership, permission, and freedom to operate distinct.

This lesson in the book, page 49 ↗

A Read the situation

A confidentiality agreement is not a material license

Two fictional research partners plan to exchange a nonclinical reference protein for three named bench assessments. A confidentiality agreement is signed, but the material-transfer terms remain a draft. Permitted use, onward transfer, publication review, and rights in new findings are unresolved. Scientist Julia asks counsel Ravi whether shipment can proceed and whether owning a related patent settles third-party rights. Their discussion identifies questions for the authorized review; it does not interpret a real agreement or approve a transfer.

Julia: Scientist / Ravi: Counsel

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. Which agreement is signed?

Answer and explanation

D. The confidentiality agreement

The brief states that confidentiality is agreed while the material-transfer terms remain a draft.

2. Which issue remains unresolved?

Answer and explanation

B. Permitted use and publication review, among other material-transfer terms

The briefing explicitly identifies the listed terms as unresolved.

3. What does ownership of a related patent establish about third-party rights here?

Answer and explanation

B. It does not by itself establish freedom to operate.

A party's own patent does not automatically resolve rights held by others.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

NDA means nondisclosure agreement; MTA means material transfer agreement; FTO means freedom to operate. Confidentiality, material permission, ownership, and third-party rights are different questions. The effect of any document depends on its actual language, scope, law, and circumstances.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
intellectual property
Legally recognized rights in inventions, works, marks, or other protected subject matter.identify relevant intellectual property
background IP
Intellectual property brought into a collaboration, as defined by the agreement.define background IP
foreground IP
New intellectual property arising from the collaboration under its agreed definitions.address foreground IP
inventorship
Legal identification of the people who contributed to a claimed invention.assess inventorship
ownership
The legal holding of a right or asset.clarify ownership
assignment
Transfer of ownership of specified rights.distinguish an assignment from a license
license
Permission to exercise specified rights under stated terms.negotiate a license
field of use
The defined application area within which permission applies.limit the field of use
territory
The geographic scope of a right or permission.specify the territory
exclusive license
Permission with defined exclusivity within its agreed scope.define exclusive-license scope
nonexclusive license
Permission that does not generally prevent granting similar rights to others.discuss a nonexclusive license
confidentiality obligation
A duty concerning protection and permitted disclosure of information.comply with confidentiality obligations
nondisclosure agreement
An agreement governing specified confidential information.review a nondisclosure agreement
material transfer agreement
An agreement governing the transfer and use of tangible research material.finalize a material transfer agreement
permitted use
The activities authorized for the material or rights under the agreement.define permitted use
onward transfer
Passing material or rights to another recipient.address onward transfer
publication review
An agreed process for reviewing proposed public disclosure.specify publication review
publication embargo
A stated restriction or delay on publication under applicable terms.define a publication embargo
patent claim
The legal wording defining the scope of protection sought or granted.review relevant patent claims
patentability
Whether an invention meets the applicable criteria for patent protection.assess patentability
freedom to operate
Whether proposed acts can proceed without infringing relevant third-party rights.obtain a scoped freedom-to-operate review
know-how
Practical technical information or expertise, sometimes protected as confidential information.identify transferred know-how
third-party rights
Rights held by someone outside the parties to the proposed arrangement.assess third-party rights
governing agreement
The contract whose applicable terms control the relevant relationship or activity.identify the governing agreement

A patent generally provides a right to exclude specified acts by others, not an automatic right to practice every related activity. Patentability and freedom to operate therefore ask different questions. Obtain appropriately scoped professional review rather than infer clearance from ownership.

C Notice the language

A shared scientific aim does not settle the agreement

Researchers may use collaborate to mean anything from a limited evaluation to joint development. Define the intended work and the relevant rights before relying on that word. Asking about publication or ownership early can protect the relationship by exposing different expectations before materials move.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Identify the signed document
The confidentiality agreement is signed; the material-transfer terms are not final.
Define the activity
The proposed use is three named nonclinical bench assessments.
Separate permissions
Permission to receive information does not necessarily authorize use of the material.
Check onward transfer
May the recipient send the material to another laboratory?
Clarify new findings
How will rights in results and new inventions be addressed?
Separate inventorship
Inventorship and ownership should not be treated as interchangeable labels.
Protect publication
What review process and time limits apply before public disclosure?
Avoid an unlimited delay
Publication review needs defined terms, not an unstated permanent veto.
Name existing rights
Identify the background IP each party brings.
Define the new work
Distinguish collaboration results from pre-existing know-how.
Ask about third parties
Which relevant rights are held outside these two parties?
Scope FTO
The review needs the proposed acts, territories, and relevant time.
Avoid a patent shortcut
Owning our patent does not automatically establish freedom to operate.
Reserve authorization
Shipment needs the appropriate completed review and authorization.
Record the open terms
Keep permitted use and publication questions visible in the draft.
Close accurately
Scientific interest is aligned; the transfer terms remain unresolved.
6 language and precision notes
Own versus use
Holding one right and having permission for a proposed activity are different questions.
NDA versus MTA
The labels indicate different purposes; read the actual terms rather than assume either is comprehensive.
Research only
Define the permitted research and any restrictions instead of relying on a broad phrase.
Publication review
Specify purpose, timing, and consequences through the actual agreement.
Inventor versus owner
The legal concepts differ and may involve different people or entities.
Cleared
State the reviewed activity and scope; do not present a limited review as universal permission.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which response best fits the shipment question?

Answer and explanation

B. The MTA terms remain unresolved, so seek the required review and authorization before treating shipment as approved.

The brief establishes only a signed confidentiality agreement and unfinished transfer terms.

2. Which review question concerns permitted use rather than a different transfer term?

Answer and explanation

C. Which named bench assessments may the recipient perform with the protein?

Permitted use defines the recipient's authorized activities. The other questions concern onward transfer, publication review, and ownership; each is important but distinct.

3. Why does a related owned patent not settle FTO?

Answer and explanation

D. Other parties may hold relevant rights affecting the proposed acts.

Rights held by others can remain relevant despite ownership of a separate patent.

4. Which statement preserves the publication issue?

Answer and explanation

B. The publication-review process and timing remain to be agreed.

The supplied draft has unresolved publication terms, so neither unrestricted release nor permanent prohibition is established.

Check explanations in the book, page 95 ↗

E Conversations

A confidentiality agreement is not a material license

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Julia: Scientist / Ravi: Counsel

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • governing agreement
  • background IP
  • nondisclosure agreement
  • material transfer agreement
  • permitted use
  • publication review
  • Inventorship
  • know-how
  • freedom to operate
  • third-party rights
  1. Julia
    The partners agree on the science, and the confidentiality agreement is signed. Can I arrange shipment of the reference protein for the three bench assessments now?
  2. Ravi
    The 1 is still a draft. We need the relevant terms reviewed and authorized before treating the transfer as approved.
  3. Julia
    I had assumed the signed confidentiality document covered the exchange. It lets us discuss the work, but that may be different from permission to use the material.
  4. Ravi
    Exactly. A 2 does not automatically answer every material-use question. We must examine the actual documents rather than assume the title grants whatever the project needs.
  5. Julia
    The intended work is limited to three named assessments. The recipient also mentioned asking a separate laboratory to help, which is not addressed in the draft.
  6. Ravi
    Define the 3 precisely and address any onward transfer. The scientific plan should match the scope being reviewed, including which organizations would actually handle the material.
  7. Julia
    We also need to clarify what happens to discoveries made during the evaluation. Each team is bringing existing methods and information to the work.
  8. Ravi
    Identify the 4 separately from new results. The definitions should let both parties distinguish their existing rights from what the collaboration may generate.
  9. Julia
    If a new invention results, naming the people who contributed is not necessarily the same as deciding which organization will own the resulting rights.
  10. Ravi
    Correct. 5 and ownership are different legal questions. We should not resolve either casually in an email saying that every new idea belongs to the team.
  11. Julia
    The recipient wants to publish. Can the draft specify how review would work, including timing, without implying either immediate unrestricted publication or an unlimited veto?
  12. Ravi
    Agree a defined 6 process with the relevant purpose, timing, and consequences. Do not assume immediate unrestricted publication or an unlimited veto when neither term has been settled.
  13. Julia
    They also asked whether access to the material means they can use our practical methods in a later unrelated project. That sounds broader than this evaluation.
  14. Ravi
    Clarify any 7 being supplied and the authorized uses. Physical possession of a reference protein does not automatically settle rights in every method discussed alongside it.
  15. Julia
    Our team owns a related patent. Some colleagues think that means we can tell the partner there are no intellectual-property obstacles to any proposed use.
  16. Ravi
    Ownership does not itself establish 8. Other rights may matter, and the review needs the actual activity, territory, and relevant circumstances before anyone makes a clearance statement.
  17. Julia
    Then our own patentability work and a third-party-rights review should not be presented as the same assessment. They answer different questions for the collaboration.
  18. Ravi
    Yes. Examine relevant 9 through appropriately scoped professional review. A right we hold is not a universal permission to practice every related technology or distribute every associated material.
  19. Julia
    I will hold shipment and send the exact assessment list and laboratory names for review. The teams agree on the science; the transfer terms still need resolution.
  20. Ravi
    That gives us a clear 10 to finalize through the proper process. Once the terms and authorization are established, the team can act within that defined scope rather than rely on assumptions.

Answers and explanations
  1. material transfer agreement Turn 2. The material transfer agreement governs the proposed tangible-material transfer and remains unfinished in this case.
  2. nondisclosure agreement Turn 4. A nondisclosure agreement addresses specified confidential information and does not automatically settle material-use permission.
  3. permitted use Turn 6. Permitted use identifies which activities the recipient is authorized to perform under the agreement.
  4. background IP Turn 8. Background IP identifies pre-existing intellectual property brought into the collaboration under the agreed definitions.
  5. Inventorship Turn 10. Inventorship identifies qualifying contributors to a claimed invention and is distinct from ownership of the rights.
  6. publication review Turn 12. Publication review is the agreed process for examining proposed disclosure, with its purpose and timing specified.
  7. know-how Turn 14. Know-how concerns practical technical information whose permitted use may differ from possession of a material.
  8. freedom to operate Turn 16. Freedom to operate concerns the proposed acts and relevant rights of others, not merely ownership of one's own patent.
  9. third-party rights Turn 18. Third-party rights are held outside these two partners and can remain relevant to the proposed activities.
  10. governing agreement Turn 20. The governing agreement supplies the actual applicable terms for the authorized collaboration rather than an informal assumption.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Julia: The partners agree on the science, and the confidentiality agreement is signed. Can I arrange shipment of the reference protein for the three bench assessments now?
  2. Ravi: The material transfer agreement is still a draft. We need the relevant terms reviewed and authorized before treating the transfer as approved.
  3. Julia: I had assumed the signed confidentiality document covered the exchange. It lets us discuss the work, but that may be different from permission to use the material.
  4. Ravi: Exactly. A nondisclosure agreement does not automatically answer every material-use question. We must examine the actual documents rather than assume the title grants whatever the project needs.
  5. Julia: The intended work is limited to three named assessments. The recipient also mentioned asking a separate laboratory to help, which is not addressed in the draft.
  6. Ravi: Define the permitted use precisely and address any onward transfer. The scientific plan should match the scope being reviewed, including which organizations would actually handle the material.
  7. Julia: We also need to clarify what happens to discoveries made during the evaluation. Each team is bringing existing methods and information to the work.
  8. Ravi: Identify the background IP separately from new results. The definitions should let both parties distinguish their existing rights from what the collaboration may generate.
  9. Julia: If a new invention results, naming the people who contributed is not necessarily the same as deciding which organization will own the resulting rights.
  10. Ravi: Correct. Inventorship and ownership are different legal questions. We should not resolve either casually in an email saying that every new idea belongs to the team.
  11. Julia: The recipient wants to publish. Can the draft specify how review would work, including timing, without implying either immediate unrestricted publication or an unlimited veto?
  12. Ravi: Agree a defined publication review process with the relevant purpose, timing, and consequences. Do not assume immediate unrestricted publication or an unlimited veto when neither term has been settled.
  13. Julia: They also asked whether access to the material means they can use our practical methods in a later unrelated project. That sounds broader than this evaluation.
  14. Ravi: Clarify any know-how being supplied and the authorized uses. Physical possession of a reference protein does not automatically settle rights in every method discussed alongside it.
  15. Julia: Our team owns a related patent. Some colleagues think that means we can tell the partner there are no intellectual-property obstacles to any proposed use.
  16. Ravi: Ownership does not itself establish freedom to operate. Other rights may matter, and the review needs the actual activity, territory, and relevant circumstances before anyone makes a clearance statement.
  17. Julia: Then our own patentability work and a third-party-rights review should not be presented as the same assessment. They answer different questions for the collaboration.
  18. Ravi: Yes. Examine relevant third-party rights through appropriately scoped professional review. A right we hold is not a universal permission to practice every related technology or distribute every associated material.
  19. Julia: I will hold shipment and send the exact assessment list and laboratory names for review. The teams agree on the science; the transfer terms still need resolution.
  20. Ravi: That gives us a clear governing agreement to finalize through the proper process. Once the terms and authorization are established, the team can act within that defined scope rather than rely on assumptions.
Dialogue in the book, page 55 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Clarify permitted use before sending materials

Fictional collaboration discussion: material exchange for assay testing is proposed, but permitted use and publication rights remain unresolved; an internal planning call is Friday.

  1. Collaboration scientist: Please arrange appropriate legal review of permitted use and publication rights before we commit to exchanging the materials.
  2. Alliance manager: I can coordinate that. Which proposed activity should the material transfer agreement review cover?
  3. Collaboration scientist: Assay testing. Please also confirm when an initial status update is feasible for Friday's planning call.
  4. Alliance manager: I'll check reviewer availability today; I can't promise the legal assessment will be complete by Friday.
  5. Collaboration scientist: A confirmed review plan would help. We need the open conditions visible before discussing transfer timing.
  6. Alliance manager: Could an email between the scientists substitute for resolving those conditions in the meantime?
  7. Collaboration scientist: We shouldn't assume that. Please keep the exchange conditional on the authorized review.
  8. Alliance manager: Understood. I'll coordinate the scoped review and return an availability update, not an approval promise.
  9. Collaboration scientist: Thank you. Material access also shouldn't be presented as a freedom-to-operate assurance.
  10. Alliance manager: Agreed. That separate question stays with the appropriate reviewers, and I'll distinguish both review statuses.
Publication review versus publication permission

Separate fictional academic-industry negotiation: one draft clause says publication review, another says prior permission; the intended arrangement is not agreed.

  1. Academic liaison: These clauses use review and prior permission differently. Could we clarify the intended publication process?
  2. Partnership manager: Yes. We shouldn't assume the two phrases create the same arrangement for the collaboration.
  3. Academic liaison: What should the legal reviewers receive so they can address the actual concern?
  4. Partnership manager: The conflicting clauses, proposed publication workflow, and each partner's stated questions about publication rights.
  5. Academic liaison: Could we tell the researchers they may publish immediately while the wording is being discussed?
  6. Partnership manager: No. We haven't confirmed the applicable arrangement or authority to make that commitment.
  7. Academic liaison: I'll request a joint clarification session before the next project planning call.
  8. Partnership manager: Please distinguish protection of confidential IP from any unagreed right to block publication.
  9. Academic liaison: I will frame both as issues for authorized review, not offer an interpretation as settled law.
  10. Partnership manager: Good. I'll coordinate the reviewers and return a confirmed process rather than leave scientists to infer the meaning.
A patent search is not a freedom-to-operate opinion

Separate fictional collaboration preparation: a quick patent-title search found no match for a proposed assay collaboration. No specialist freedom-to-operate review has occurred; the partner planning call is Thursday.

  1. Research director: Our quick title search found no match. Can we tell the partner there is no IP risk?
  2. Technology transfer officer: No. That search is not a freedom-to-operate assessment, and titles alone don't settle the question.
  3. Research director: Please arrange qualified review of the proposed assay collaboration and confirm what scope information the reviewer needs.
  4. Technology transfer officer: I'll coordinate that request. Please provide the current activity description rather than just the search result.
  5. Research director: I'll provide it today. Could you confirm when an initial review-plan update is feasible before Thursday's call?
  6. Technology transfer officer: I can check availability today, but I can't promise a completed opinion or favorable conclusion by Thursday.
  7. Research director: Understood. The immediate request is a scoped review plan and timing estimate, not a clearance statement.
  8. Technology transfer officer: Yes. I'll return the coordination update and flag any missing information for the reviewer.
  9. Research director: I'll keep the partner message clear that the preliminary search doesn't establish freedom to operate.
  10. Technology transfer officer: Good. Review coordination is accepted; the legal outcome remains unconfirmed.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

A confidentiality agreement is not a material license
  1. Read the transfer review. Distinguish the signed confidentiality agreement from the unfinished material-transfer terms.
  2. Swap roles. Separate permitted use, onward transfer, publication review, and ownership; retain the unapproved shipment status.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Access to a file is not every right

A partner may receive a confidential method summary for evaluation. No permission for commercial use or onward disclosure has been agreed in the supplied facts.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Scientist: "The stated purpose is ___."

Answer and explanation

C. evaluation

The supplied permission concerns evaluating the confidential method summary.

2. Partner: "Commercial use is not established as ___."

Answer and explanation

B. permitted

The brief expressly says commercial-use permission has not been agreed.

3. Scientist: "Onward disclosure also remains ___."

Answer and explanation

C. unresolved

No onward-disclosure permission is supplied, so it cannot be assumed.

4. Partner: "We need the actual governing ___."

Answer and explanation

C. terms

The relevant permissions depend on the applicable agreement rather than access alone.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Lesson 06: IP, Freedom to Operate, and Collaboration
Original fictional case: Two fictional research partners plan to exchange a nonclinical reference protein for three named bench assessments. A confidentiality agreement is signed, but the material-transfer terms remain a draft. Permitted use, onward transfer, publication review, and rights in new findings are unresolved. Scientist Julia asks counsel Ravi whether shipment can proceed and whether owning a related patent settles third-party rights. Their discussion identifies questions for the authorized review; it does not interpret a real agreement or approve a transfer.
Language workshop: A confidentiality agreement is not a material license
Communication goal: Identify unresolved material-use and publication terms while keeping ownership, permission, and freedom to operate distinct.
Language guidance: NDA means nondisclosure agreement; MTA means material transfer agreement; FTO means freedom to operate. Confidentiality, material permission, ownership, and third-party rights are different questions. The effect of any document depends on its actual language, scope, law, and circumstances.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): The confidentiality agreement is signed; the material-transfer terms are not final. / The proposed use is three named nonclinical bench assessments. / Permission to receive information does not necessarily authorize use of the material. / May the recipient send the material to another laboratory? / How will rights in results and new inventions be addressed? / Inventorship and ownership should not be treated as interchangeable labels. / What review process and time limits apply before public disclosure? / Publication review needs defined terms, not an unstated permanent veto. / Identify the background IP each party brings. / Distinguish collaboration results from pre-existing know-how. / Which relevant rights are held outside these two parties? / The review needs the proposed acts, territories, and relevant time. / Owning our patent does not automatically establish freedom to operate. / Shipment needs the appropriate completed review and authorization. / Keep permitted use and publication questions visible in the draft. / Scientific interest is aligned; the transfer terms remain unresolved.
Vocabulary: intellectual property: Legally recognized rights in inventions, works, marks, or other protected subject matter. / background IP: Intellectual property brought into a collaboration, as defined by the agreement. / foreground IP: New intellectual property arising from the collaboration under its agreed definitions. / inventorship: Legal identification of the people who contributed to a claimed invention. / ownership: The legal holding of a right or asset. / assignment: Transfer of ownership of specified rights. / license: Permission to exercise specified rights under stated terms. / field of use: The defined application area within which permission applies. / territory: The geographic scope of a right or permission. / exclusive license: Permission with defined exclusivity within its agreed scope. / nonexclusive license: Permission that does not generally prevent granting similar rights to others. / confidentiality obligation: A duty concerning protection and permitted disclosure of information. / nondisclosure agreement: An agreement governing specified confidential information. / material transfer agreement: An agreement governing the transfer and use of tangible research material. / permitted use: The activities authorized for the material or rights under the agreement. / onward transfer: Passing material or rights to another recipient. / publication review: An agreed process for reviewing proposed public disclosure. / publication embargo: A stated restriction or delay on publication under applicable terms. / patent claim: The legal wording defining the scope of protection sought or granted. / patentability: Whether an invention meets the applicable criteria for patent protection. / freedom to operate: Whether proposed acts can proceed without infringing relevant third-party rights. / know-how: Practical technical information or expertise, sometimes protected as confidential information. / third-party rights: Rights held by someone outside the parties to the proposed arrangement. / governing agreement: The contract whose applicable terms control the relevant relationship or activity.
Speaking task: Read the transfer review. Distinguish the signed confidentiality agreement from the unfinished material-transfer terms. Swap roles. Separate permitted use, onward transfer, publication review, and ownership; retain the unapproved shipment status. Read the corrected file-access transfer. Keep evaluation permission distinct from commercial use and onward disclosure.
Partner: Julia: Scientist / Ravi: Counsel
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A partner may receive a confidential method summary for evaluation. No permission for commercial use or onward disclosure has been agreed in the supplied facts.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Julia: Then our own patentability work and a third-party-rights review should not be presented as the same assessment. They answer different questions for the collaboration.
Ravi: Yes. Examine relevant third-party rights through appropriately scoped professional review. A right we hold is not a universal permission to practice every related technology or distribute every associated material.
Julia: I will hold shipment and send the exact assessment list and laboratory names for review. The teams agree on the science; the transfer terms still need resolution.
Ravi: That gives us a clear governing agreement to finalize through the proper process. Once the terms and authorization are established, the team can act within that defined scope rather than rely on assumptions.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

07Investor and Board UpdatesSix experiments, one unfinished milestone+

Your goal

Connect completed work to a scientific funding criterion and explain a conditional cash-runway calculation.

This lesson in the book, page 58 ↗

A Read the situation

Six experiments, one unfinished milestone

Program lead Daria and finance lead Miles prepare a fictional board update. Four binding experiments and two functional runs are complete. The agreed next funding criterion requires three consistent independent functional runs; the third is pending. Unrestricted cash is $12 million and the base-case net cash burn is $1.2 million per month. A proposed $0.3 million follow-up budget is not yet approved or included in that base case. The board needs milestone status and a bounded decision request, not a count presented as proof.

Daria: Program lead / Miles: Finance lead

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. Why is the funding criterion not yet met?

Answer and explanation

A. Only two of the required three independent functional runs are complete.

The agreed criterion requires three consistent functional runs, and the third remains pending.

2. What is the simple base-case runway at unchanged net burn?

Answer and explanation

B. Ten months

Twelve million divided by one point two million per month equals ten months.

3. How should the $0.3 million proposal be described?

Answer and explanation

C. Additional proposed funding, not yet approved or included in the base case

The brief explicitly leaves the proposed budget unapproved and outside the current base case.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Six completed experiments do not satisfy a criterion requiring three consistent independent functional runs when only two are complete. The category and quality of evidence matter. A milestone is not met merely because the activity count looks substantial.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
milestone criterion
The evidence or condition required to reach a defined project milestone.specify the milestone criterion
readout
The reporting of results from a study or experiment.schedule a data readout
evidence package
The organized results and supporting records used for a decision.assemble the evidence package
go/no-go decision
A defined decision to continue, stop, or redirect a program.support a go/no-go decision
scientific risk
Uncertainty about whether the underlying biological or technical premise will hold.reduce scientific risk
execution risk
Uncertainty about delivering the planned work as intended.manage execution risk
critical path
The sequence of dependent tasks determining the earliest completion point.identify the critical path
dependency
An input or task that another activity relies on.make dependencies explicit
board ask
The specific decision or support requested from the board.state the board ask
resource allocation
Assignment of money, people, or capacity to defined work.justify resource allocation
unrestricted cash
Cash available for use without the specified restrictions of restricted funds.report unrestricted cash
net cash burn
Net cash outflow over a stated period.calculate monthly net cash burn
gross cash burn
Total cash outflow before offsetting inflows over a stated period.distinguish gross cash burn
cash runway
The estimated period before available cash is exhausted under stated assumptions.estimate cash runway
run rate
A current rate projected over another period under stated assumptions.qualify the run-rate estimate
base case
The central planning scenario used for comparison.update the base case
downside case
A planning scenario with less favorable specified assumptions.test the downside case
budget variance
The difference between planned and actual spending on the same basis.explain a budget variance
contingent funding
Funding dependent on specified future conditions.identify contingent funding
financing round
A defined transaction or process to raise capital.prepare for a financing round
dilution
Reduction in an existing owner's proportional interest following additional equity issuance.explain potential dilution
inflection point
A development event expected to materially change a program's position or perceived value.define the proposed inflection point
milestone payment
A payment triggered by a specified achievement under an agreement.verify milestone-payment conditions
decision record
A record of the decision, basis, conditions, and responsible parties.maintain a decision record

The simple runway is $12 million divided by $1.2 million per month: ten months at unchanged net burn. This is a conditional illustration, not a forecast guarantee. New spending, timing, inflows, or restrictions require an updated cash model.

C Notice the language

A board update should connect evidence and choice

Senior audiences often ask whether a program is de-risked. Identify which uncertainty has been reduced and which criterion remains open. A clear request names the proposed work and resources without presenting a favorable experimental outcome or future funding as guaranteed.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Lead with the decision
We are asking for a bounded follow-up budget, not declaring the milestone complete.
Report the work
Four binding experiments and two functional runs are complete.
Name the criterion
The gate requires three consistent independent functional runs.
State the gap
The third functional run is pending.
Separate evidence types
Binding data support the program but do not replace the functional criterion.
Avoid total de-risking
One uncertainty may be reduced while other risks remain.
Connect the spend
The proposed budget supports the next defined evidence question.
State approval status
The additional $0.3 million has not yet been approved.
Show the runway basis
At unchanged net burn, twelve divided by one point two gives ten months.
Keep the model current
The new proposal is outside the current base case.
Separate cash and science
Available cash does not establish a successful scientific result.
Name the dependency
The funding decision depends on the evidence criterion, not only the calendar.
Use a downside case
Show what changes if the result or timing is less favorable.
Avoid a funding promise
A planned financing round is not cash already received.
Record the conditions
Capture the board's actual decision and any conditions.
Close with the next readout
Return with the third-run result and the updated cash implications.
6 language and precision notes
Completed
Name the work completed rather than imply every milestone is complete.
De-risked
Specify the risk reduced; do not imply that uncertainty disappeared.
Runway
State cash, burn basis, and assumptions.
Funded
Distinguish a proposal, approval, contractual commitment, and cash received.
Milestone
Use the agreed criterion, not a convenient substitute created for the update.
Base case
An unapproved new cost is not automatically already included.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which opening is most decision-ready?

Answer and explanation

A. Two functional runs are complete; the third required by the funding criterion is pending, and we request $0.3 million for the defined follow-up.

The opening connects actual evidence, the unmet criterion, and the specific proposed decision.

2. What makes the ten-month runway conditional?

Answer and explanation

D. It assumes unchanged net burn and the stated available cash basis.

The arithmetic holds only under the cash and burn assumptions stated in the brief.

3. The board packet counts six completed experiments against the three-run gate. What is the reporting error?

Answer and explanation

C. It combines different evidence categories although the criterion specifies functional runs.

Four binding experiments plus two functional runs is six activities, but only two match the specified category. The third functional run is still pending; a total count does not satisfy the gate.

4. What should happen if the new budget is approved?

Answer and explanation

D. Update the cash model for the actual spending assumptions and record the decision.

An approved additional spend changes planning inputs without establishing the experimental outcome.

E Conversations

Six experiments, one unfinished milestone

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Daria: Program lead / Miles: Finance lead

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • decision record
  • base case
  • board ask
  • evidence package
  • milestone criterion
  • downside case
  • cash runway
  • contingent funding
  • scientific risk
  • dependency
  1. Daria
    The update opens with six experiments completed. That is accurate as an activity count, but I am concerned it makes the next funding milestone sound finished.
  2. Miles
    State the 1 before the count. The agreed requirement is three consistent independent functional runs, and we currently have two with the third still pending.
  3. Daria
    Four of the six completed experiments are binding work. They support the program, but they do not replace the particular functional evidence required at this gate.
  4. Miles
    Exactly. The 2 should separate those categories. The board needs to see which result addresses which criterion, not infer progress from a single large total.
  5. Daria
    We also need to revise the phrase fully de-risked. The completed work may reduce some uncertainty, but the pending functional result could still affect the direction.
  6. Miles
    Name the remaining 3. Do not turn progress into a claim that every uncertainty has disappeared, particularly when the agreed evidence condition remains open.
  7. Daria
    My request is the additional three-hundred-thousand-dollar budget for the defined follow-up. I am not asking for an unlimited commitment to every later development stage.
  8. Miles
    Make that the 4. State the work it supports and its approval status. At present the amount is proposed, not approved or already incorporated into the base case.
  9. Daria
    The finance panel shows twelve million dollars of unrestricted cash and net burn of one point two million a month. That gives ten months under unchanged assumptions.
  10. Miles
    Yes, as a simple 5 illustration. Put the assumptions next to the number so it is not heard as a guarantee that every future spending plan is already covered.
  11. Daria
    Show the proposed three hundred thousand separately. What happens to the forecast if the board approves it, given that the base case excludes it?
  12. Miles
    Update the 6 if the board approves new spending, using the actual timing assumptions. Approval changes the plan; it does not make the original calculation timeless.
  13. Daria
    There is also a proposed financing round later. We should keep that distinct from cash already available rather than use it to make the runway look longer today.
  14. Miles
    Correct. Treat any 7 according to its actual status. A plan to raise money is not a receipt, and an unmet scientific condition should remain visible.
  15. Daria
    For the schedule, the third functional run is the key unresolved item. The board should understand how its outcome connects to the next program decision.
  16. Miles
    Identify that 8 explicitly. The calendar alone does not satisfy an evidence gate, and a completed run still needs its result evaluated against the criterion.
  17. Daria
    We can also show what happens if the result does not support continuation or if the timing moves. That makes the spending choice more concrete.
  18. Miles
    Use a stated 9 without presenting it as a prediction. The purpose is to show which assumptions change the resource choice and when the board would revisit it.
  19. Daria
    I will put the third functional run and the budget request on the decision slide. Please add when the board will see the result and updated cash position.
  20. Miles
    Capture the actual approval or deferral in the 10. Keep funding authorization, experiment completion, and a successful scientific result distinct, even on the same slide.

Answers and explanations
  1. milestone criterion Turn 2. The milestone criterion specifies three consistent independent functional runs rather than a total activity count.
  2. evidence package Turn 4. The evidence package organizes the relevant results for the decision without treating unlike experiments as interchangeable.
  3. scientific risk Turn 6. Scientific risk concerns whether the biological or technical premise will hold, which completed activity alone cannot eliminate.
  4. board ask Turn 8. The board ask is the specific bounded funding decision being requested, distinct from reporting completed work.
  5. cash runway Turn 10. Cash runway estimates the duration of available cash under the stated net-burn assumptions.
  6. base case Turn 12. The base case is the current central planning scenario, which excludes the unapproved additional proposal here.
  7. contingent funding Turn 14. Contingent funding depends on future conditions and is not equivalent to cash already received.
  8. dependency Turn 16. A dependency identifies the evidence or task on which the next decision relies.
  9. downside case Turn 18. A downside case shows the consequences of specified less-favorable assumptions without treating them as established outcomes.
  10. decision record Turn 20. The decision record preserves what the board actually decided and any conditions attached to it.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Daria: The update opens with six experiments completed. That is accurate as an activity count, but I am concerned it makes the next funding milestone sound finished.
  2. Miles: State the milestone criterion before the count. The agreed requirement is three consistent independent functional runs, and we currently have two with the third still pending.
  3. Daria: Four of the six completed experiments are binding work. They support the program, but they do not replace the particular functional evidence required at this gate.
  4. Miles: Exactly. The evidence package should separate those categories. The board needs to see which result addresses which criterion, not infer progress from a single large total.
  5. Daria: We also need to revise the phrase fully de-risked. The completed work may reduce some uncertainty, but the pending functional result could still affect the direction.

  7. Daria: My request is the additional three-hundred-thousand-dollar budget for the defined follow-up. I am not asking for an unlimited commitment to every later development stage.
  8. Miles: Make that the board ask. State the work it supports and its approval status. At present the amount is proposed, not approved or already incorporated into the base case.

Dialogue in the book, page 64 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Completed experiments versus the funding milestone

Fictional board preparation: three assay runs at site A met the within-site criterion. The next funding-review milestone requires reviewed evidence from both sites A and B; site B has not run the assay, and funding is not guaranteed.

  1. Chief executive: Three runs met the criterion at site A. Have we reached the funding milestone?
  2. Program lead: Not yet. The milestone requires reviewed evidence from both sites, and site B hasn't run the assay.
  3. Chief executive: So completed within-site work is progress, but it doesn't establish between-site performance?
  4. Program lead: Exactly. Site A's result addresses one question; the second site's evidence remains missing.
  5. Chief executive: Can I describe this as all technical risk removed before the board discusses funding?
  6. Program lead: No. That would hide the untested site and overstate what those three runs establish.
  7. Chief executive: Then today's status is site A criterion met, site B work pending, milestone not yet achieved.
  8. Program lead: Correct. Completing the eventual milestone would support funding review, not guarantee financing or extend runway automatically.
  9. Chief executive: I'll separate the scientific achievement, remaining evidence, and financial assumptions in the update.
  10. Program lead: I'll bring the site B planning questions so the board can discuss the next decision with that gap visible.
A milestone reached with a remaining uncertainty

Separate fictional board exercise: a predefined assay milestone is achieved, but between-site performance remains untested and was outside that milestone's scope.

  1. Board presenter: We achieved the assay milestone. Can I say the program's technical risk has been removed?
  2. Research lead: No. That milestone covered a defined scope; between-site performance remains untested.
  3. Board presenter: So achieving the milestone and completing all risk mitigation are not the same statement?
  4. Research lead: Exactly. A milestone can be achieved while important follow-up questions remain.
  5. Board presenter: How do I describe the achievement without letting the limitation erase its value?
  6. Research lead: State the agreed criterion was met, then explain the next unresolved question and its significance.
  7. Board presenter: Could we call it an inflection point if the board hasn't discussed the decision impact?
  8. Research lead: Explain the impact first. Don't use the label as a substitute for the actual change in evidence.
  9. Board presenter: I'll show the achieved milestone beside the remaining between-site assessment.
  10. Research lead: I'll prepare that distinction and keep any next funding decision separate from the completed scientific criterion.
Runway under two spending assumptions

Separate fictional finance rehearsal: current cash supports nine months at one planned spending rate and six months at a higher proposed rate; neither plan is selected.

  1. Scientific founder: Can the investor update simply say we have nine months of runway?
  2. Finance director: Only with its assumption. The higher proposed spending rate gives six months, and no plan is selected.
  3. Scientific founder: Is runway a fixed date in the accounts rather than a planning estimate?
  4. Finance director: It is an estimate tied to cash and spending assumptions, so the stated scenario matters.
  5. Scientific founder: Could we present nine months as a commitment while describing the expanded experiments separately?
  6. Finance director: That would disconnect the activity plan from its financial assumption and could mislead the audience.
  7. Scientific founder: I'll explain both scenarios and their relationship to the next scientific milestone.
  8. Finance director: Good. Don't assume that reaching that milestone guarantees new financing in either scenario.
  9. Scientific founder: I'll keep funding prospects separate and ask the board which plan it wants to consider.
  10. Finance director: I'll provide the scoped estimates and identify the assumptions that need updating before a decision.

New spending changes the assumptions

A fictional program has $9 million of available cash and a steady net burn of $1.5 million per month. A new project cost is proposed but excluded from that base case.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Finance lead: "The simple base-case runway is ___ months."

Answer and explanation

C. six

Nine million divided by one point five million per month equals six months.

2. Program lead: "That assumes an unchanged net ___."

Answer and explanation

A. burn

The calculation holds only at the stated net cash-outflow rate.

3. Finance lead: "The new project cost is currently ___."

Answer and explanation

A. excluded

The briefing states that the proposed cost is outside the base case.

4. Program lead: "Approval would require an updated cash ___."

Answer and explanation

C. model

Adding approved spending requires the planning assumptions and resulting cash estimate to be updated.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Optional second-round challenge: A fictional program has $9 million of available cash and a steady net burn of $1.5 million per month. A new project cost is proposed but excluded from that base case.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Daria: We can also show what happens if the result does not support continuation or if the timing moves. That makes the spending choice more concrete.
Miles: Use a stated downside case without presenting it as a prediction. The purpose is to show which assumptions change the resource choice and when the board would revisit it.
Daria: I will put the third functional run and the budget request on the decision slide. Please add when the board will see the result and updated cash position.
Miles: Capture the actual approval or deferral in the decision record. Keep funding authorization, experiment completion, and a successful scientific result distinct, even on the same slide.
END REFERENCE
Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. REFERENCE
Course: Biotechnology English
Professional audience: biotech scientists, translational researchers, assay-development teams, platform teams, program managers, alliance managers, business-development staff, and biotech executives

Lesson 07: Investor and Board Updates
Original fictional case: Program lead Daria and finance lead Miles prepare a fictional board update. Four binding experiments and two functional runs are complete. The agreed next funding criterion requires three consistent independent functional runs; the third is pending. Unrestricted cash is $12 million and the base-case net cash burn is $1.2 million per month. A proposed $0.3 million follow-up budget is not yet approved or included in that base case. The board needs milestone status and a bounded decision request, not a count presented as proof.
Language workshop: Six experiments, one unfinished milestone
Communication goal: Connect completed work to a scientific funding criterion and explain a conditional cash-runway calculation.
Language guidance: Six completed experiments do not satisfy a criterion requiring three consistent independent functional runs when only two are complete. The category and quality of evidence matter. A milestone is not met merely because the activity count looks substantial.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): We are asking for a bounded follow-up budget, not declaring the milestone complete. / Four binding experiments and two functional runs are complete. / The gate requires three consistent independent functional runs. / The third functional run is pending. / Binding data support the program but do not replace the functional criterion. / One uncertainty may be reduced while other risks remain. / The proposed budget supports the next defined evidence question. / The additional $0.3 million has not yet been approved. / At unchanged net burn, twelve divided by one point two gives ten months. / The new proposal is outside the current base case. / Available cash does not establish a successful scientific result. / The funding decision depends on the evidence criterion, not only the calendar. / Show what changes if the result or timing is less favorable. / A planned financing round is not cash already received. / Capture the board's actual decision and any conditions. / Return with the third-run result and the updated cash implications.
Vocabulary: milestone criterion: The evidence or condition required to reach a defined project milestone. / readout: The reporting of results from a study or experiment. / evidence package: The organized results and supporting records used for a decision. / go/no-go decision: A defined decision to continue, stop, or redirect a program. / scientific risk: Uncertainty about whether the underlying biological or technical premise will hold. / execution risk: Uncertainty about delivering the planned work as intended. / critical path: The sequence of dependent tasks determining the earliest completion point. / dependency: An input or task that another activity relies on. / board ask: The specific decision or support requested from the board. / resource allocation: Assignment of money, people, or capacity to defined work. / unrestricted cash: Cash available for use without the specified restrictions of restricted funds. / net cash burn: Net cash outflow over a stated period. / gross cash burn: Total cash outflow before offsetting inflows over a stated period. / cash runway: The estimated period before available cash is exhausted under stated assumptions. / run rate: A current rate projected over another period under stated assumptions. / base case: The central planning scenario used for comparison. / downside case: A planning scenario with less favorable specified assumptions. / budget variance: The difference between planned and actual spending on the same basis. / contingent funding: Funding dependent on specified future conditions. / financing round: A defined transaction or process to raise capital. / dilution: Reduction in an existing owner's proportional interest following additional equity issuance. / inflection point: A development event expected to materially change a program's position or perceived value. / milestone payment: A payment triggered by a specified achievement under an agreement. / decision record: A record of the decision, basis, conditions, and responsible parties.
Speaking task: Read the board update. Report four binding experiments and two functional runs, with the third functional run pending. Swap roles. State ten months at the base-case burn and the unapproved $0.3 million outside that model. Read the corrected cash transfer. Use nine divided by one point five: six months before any new project cost.
Partner: Daria: Program lead / Miles: Finance lead
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A fictional program has $9 million of available cash and a steady net burn of $1.5 million per month. A new project cost is proposed but excluded from that base case.

08Partnering and Business DevelopmentDefine the collaboration before granting exclusivity+

Your goal

Clarify the scope and conditions of a proposed partnership without confusing a discussion, an option, and a granted license.

This lesson in the book, page 67 ↗

A Read the situation

Define the collaboration before granting exclusivity

Biotech partnering lead Nina meets prospective partner Tomas. The proposed first stage is a six-month nonclinical evaluation of two fictional targets, T2 and T5. Tomas requests worldwide exclusive rights across all platform applications before the collaboration scope is defined. No license, option, budget, or governance terms are approved. Nina is authorized to discuss a narrower proposal, not grant rights. They must identify the scope, evidence, decision process, and unresolved commercial terms for a reviewed term sheet.

Nina: Biotech partnering lead / Tomas: Prospective partner

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What is the proposed first-stage work?

Answer and explanation

C. A six-month nonclinical evaluation of T2 and T5

The stated initial proposal is limited to two targets and a six-month nonclinical evaluation.

2. What authority does Nina have?

Answer and explanation

A. Discuss a narrower proposal, not grant rights

The brief expressly limits Nina to discussion rather than granting rights.

3. What remains unapproved?

Answer and explanation

C. License, option, budget, and governance terms

The briefing identifies those substantive agreement terms as not approved.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

A proposal to discuss exclusivity does not grant it. A term sheet is not automatically entirely nonbinding; its effect depends on the actual wording and law. An option to obtain rights is also distinct from an exercised option or an existing license grant.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
partnering thesis
The argument for why a collaboration could create value for both parties.articulate the partnering thesis
strategic fit
Alignment between the collaboration and the parties' capabilities or goals.assess strategic fit
evaluation license
Permission limited to a defined assessment purpose under agreed terms.scope an evaluation license
option agreement
An agreement giving a defined opportunity to obtain specified rights on stated conditions.negotiate an option agreement
option exercise
The act of using an option according to its agreed conditions.define option-exercise requirements
license grant
The clause or act conveying specified permission under a license.delimit the license grant
exclusivity scope
The defined activities, rights, territory, and period covered by exclusivity.narrow the exclusivity scope
field restriction
A limitation to specified applications or uses.define a field restriction
territorial restriction
A geographic limit on the relevant rights or activities.clarify territorial restrictions
reserved rights
Rights explicitly retained rather than granted to the other party.identify reserved rights
sublicensing
Granting another party rights under authority provided by a license.address sublicensing permission
diligence obligation
An agreed requirement to make specified efforts or progress.negotiate diligence obligations
development milestone
A defined development achievement linked to review or agreed consequences.specify development milestones
upfront payment
Consideration due at an initial agreed point in a transaction.distinguish an upfront payment
royalty
A payment calculated on an agreed basis for use of licensed rights.define the royalty basis
milestone consideration
Payment or value contingent on a specified contractual achievement.clarify milestone consideration
cost sharing
Allocation of specified expenses between collaborating parties.agree cost sharing
joint steering committee
A joint body with defined oversight or coordination responsibilities.establish a joint steering committee
governance
The agreed structures and rules for overseeing a collaboration.define collaboration governance
decision right
Authority to make a specified decision.allocate decision rights
termination right
A contractual ability to end an arrangement under defined conditions.review termination rights
rights reversion
Return or cessation of specified granted rights under agreed conditions.define rights reversion
data room
An organized controlled-access collection of information for review.maintain the data room
term sheet
A document summarizing proposed principal deal terms, with legal effect depending on its wording.prepare a reviewed term sheet

Scope includes more than the word exclusive: identify the rights, field, territory, duration, conditions, and retained uses. Governance also needs actual decision authority. A committee name alone does not establish who may approve a budget, change the program, or license an asset.

C Notice the language

Explore interests before accepting the broad wording

A request for exclusivity may reflect a concern about investing resources while a competitor receives the same opportunity. Acknowledge that concern, then clarify the protected activity and duration. A narrower proposal can keep negotiation moving without implying that rights have already been granted.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Acknowledge the interest
I understand that you want protection for the resources you would commit.
Define the first stage
The proposed evaluation covers T2 and T5 for six months.
Challenge the breadth
All platform applications is broader than the work currently proposed.
Separate discussion from grant
I can discuss scope, but I cannot grant rights in this meeting.
Ask about the field
Which specific applications need exclusivity for the evaluation?
Clarify duration
What period would the proposed restriction cover?
Protect retained uses
We need to identify the rights and activities each party retains.
Distinguish the option
A future licensing option is not the same as a present commercial license.
Define the trigger
Which evidence and conditions would permit an option to be exercised?
Connect commitment
Any exclusivity proposal needs appropriately defined obligations and review.
Clarify funding
Which costs would each party bear during the evaluation?
Ask about governance
Who would have authority over scope, budget, and continuation decisions?
Avoid assumed agreement
Those terms are still open and subject to the required review.
Limit the information
Provide data-room access within the agreed confidentiality and use conditions.
Check the document
Identify which term-sheet provisions, if any, are intended to bind the parties.
Close the meeting
We will prepare a narrower proposal without representing that rights are already granted.
6 language and precision notes
Exclusive
Incomplete without the rights, activity, place, time, and conditions.
Option versus license
The ability to obtain rights later is not the same as already holding them.
Partner
A commercial relationship label does not define decision authority.
Worldwide
Geographic breadth does not specify the field of use.
Milestone payment
The trigger comes from actual agreed terms, not any favorable laboratory result.
Agreed to discuss
A process commitment should not be recorded as a completed rights grant.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which counterproposal fits Nina's authority?

Answer and explanation

C. Prepare a reviewed proposal limited to the two-target, six-month evaluation and explicitly unresolved terms.

Nina may discuss and prepare a narrower proposal but is not authorized to grant rights.

2. Why distinguish an option from a license?

Answer and explanation

B. An option concerns a defined opportunity to obtain rights, while a license grants specified permission.

The two arrangements confer different positions, and the actual terms determine their scope and exercise.

3. A draft names a joint steering committee but gives it no powers. Which question addresses that gap?

Answer and explanation

D. Who may approve scope changes, budgets, and continuation decisions?

Governance requires actual decision authority and rules. Targets, territory, and data access are separate scope questions and do not establish what the committee may decide.

4. What should the meeting record say?

Answer and explanation

B. The parties will prepare a narrower proposal; no rights grant is approved.

The record must preserve discussion status and the absence of an authorized grant.

  4. Nina
    A defined 2 could keep the first stage aligned with the actual work. Broader applications require their own review; I cannot grant platform-wide rights in this meeting.
  5. Tomas
    Could we start with evaluation rights and secure a route to a later commercial license if the results meet agreed criteria?
  6. Nina
    We can discuss an 3 as a proposal. It would need clear scope, conditions, and approval; a possible future option is not already a commercial license.
  7. Tomas
    We would need to know what evidence qualifies and when we must decide. Otherwise the option could be too uncertain to support a meaningful investment.
  8. Nina
    Define the 4 requirements alongside the evidence criteria. We should also distinguish scientific success from the separate commercial and legal conditions for obtaining rights.
  9. Tomas
    What happens to your own research during the evaluation? We do not necessarily need to stop unrelated platform work, but we need to understand competing activity.
  10. Nina
    Identify the 5 and proposed restrictions explicitly. Neither side should infer a platform-wide freeze from a limited evaluation, nor assume an exception that the terms do not state.
  11. Tomas
    We can bring specialist capability and funding for the evaluation, but the division of costs has not been discussed. That needs to be part of the same proposal.
  12. Nina
    Yes, specify 6 for the actual work. A broad expression of interest should not conceal who is paying for which activities or approving additional spending.
  13. Tomas
    If we seek some exclusivity, you will want assurance that the work moves forward. We should discuss what commitments would accompany that protection.
  14. Nina
    Appropriate 7 terms can address that, subject to review. The proposal should state the expected progress and consequences rather than rely on a general promise to try.
  15. Tomas
    Could a joint committee handle routine technical decisions? I do not want a small adjustment to require another executive negotiation every time.
  16. Nina

  17. Tomas
